We prove that the element h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} is a permanent cycle in the Adams spectral sequence. As a result, we establish the existence of smooth framed manifolds with Kervaire invariant one in dimension 126, thereby resolving the final case of the Kervaire invariant problem. Combining this result with the theorems of Browder, Mahowald–Tangora, Barratt–Jones–Mahowald, and Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel, we conclude that smooth framed manifolds with Kervaire invariant one exist in and only in dimensions 2 , 6 , 14 , 30 , 62 2,6,14,30,62 , and 126 126 . 1. Introduction For a smooth framed manifold in dimension 4 ​ k + 2 4k+2 , the Kervaire invariant, taking values in 𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2} , determines whether the manifold could be converted into a homotopy sphere via surgery – it takes value 0 if the manifold can be converted to a homotopy sphere and 1 otherwise. Formally speaking, the Kervaire invariant is defined as the Arf invariant of the quadratic refinement of the intersection pairing in the cohomology of the manifold with 𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2} coefficients. Using this invariant, Kervaire [ 33 ] discovered a PL-manifold in dimension 10 that does not admit any smooth structure. The Kervaire invariant problem seeks to identify the dimensions in which there exist framed smooth manifolds with Kervaire invariant one. In these dimensions, exactly half of the cobordism classes of framed manifolds have Kervaire invariant one, and the other half have Kervaire invariant 0. The Kervaire invariant problem is closely related to many problems in differential topology, especially Kervaire–Milnor’s classification theorem [ 34 ] on exotic smooth structures on spheres. In this paper we prove the following Theorem 1.1 . Theorem 1.1 . There exist framed manifolds of Kervaire invariant one in dimension 126. Together with previous results by Browder [ 10 ] , Mahowald–Tangora [ 44 ] , Barratt–Jones–Mahowald [ 4 ] , and Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel [ 23 ] , this is the last case of the Kervaire invariant problem. Corollary 1.2 . The dimensions that there exist framed manifolds of Kervaire invariant one are 2, 6, 14, 30, 62, and 126. In dimensions 2, 6, and 14, the product of spheres S 1 × S 1 , S 3 × S 3 , S 7 × S 7 S^{1}\times S^{1},\ S^{3}\times S^{3},S^{7}\times S^{7} can be framed to have Kervarie invariant one. In dimension 30, an explicit framed manifold of Kervarie invariant one was constructed by J.Jones in [ 32 ] . For dimensions 62 and 126, we would like to comment that no explicit manifold of Kervarie invariant one was known, although 50 % 50\% of all framed manifolds in these two dimensions have Kervarie invariant one. Our Theorem 1.1 is a consequence of Browder’s theorem (Theorem 1.3 ) and the following main theorem of this paper, Theorem 1.4 . Theorem 1.3 (Browder [ 10 ] ) . There exist framed manifolds in dimension n n of Kervaire invariant one if and only if (1) n = 2 j + 1 − 2 n=2^{j+1}-2 , and (2) the element h j 2 h_{j}^{2} in the Adams spectral sequence survives to the E ∞ E_{\infty} -page. The Adams spectral sequence [ 1 ] converges to the stable homotopy groups of spheres, which, by Pontryagin’s theorem, correspond to framed manifolds up to framed cobordism. When the element h j 2 h_{j}^{2} survives (see below for an introduction to the Adams spectral sequence and the element h j 2 h_{j}^{2} ), the homotopy classes it detects are denoted by θ j ∈ π 2 j + 1 − 2 \theta_{j}\in\pi_{2^{j+1}-2} . Browder showed that these classes correspond to framed manifolds with Kervaire invariant one. Theorem 1.4 (Theorem 7.1 ) . The element h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} survives to the E ∞ E_{\infty} -page in the Adams spectral sequence. In addition to Theorem 1.1 , further implications of Theorem 1.4 , particularly in manifold topology and unstable homotopy theory, can be found in [ 47 , 42 , 23 , 3 ] , among others. Recall that the 2-primary Adams spectral sequence has the following form. E 2 s , t = Ext A s , t ​ ( 𝔽 2 , 𝔽 2 ) ⟹ π t − s ​ S 0 , E_{2}^{s,t}=\mathrm{Ext}^{s,t}_{A}(\mathbb{F}_{2},\mathbb{F}_{2})\Longrightarrow\pi_{t-s}{S^{0}}, d r : E r s , t ⟶ E r s + r , t + r − 1 . d_{r}:E_{r}^{s,t}\longrightarrow E_{r}^{s+r,t+r-1}. Here the E 2 E_{2} -page is the cohomology of the mod 2 Steenrod algebra A, S 0 S^{0} is the 2-completed sphere spectrum, and the spectral sequence converges to the 2-completed stable homotopy groups of spheres. In general, for a spectrum X X , its H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -Adams spectral sequence is denoted by E r ∗ , ∗ ​ ( X ) E_{r}^{*,*}(X) , converging to the 2-completed homotopy groups of X X . For the sphere, Adams [ 1 ] computed the Adams 1 1 -line: Ext A 1 , t ​ ( 𝔽 2 , 𝔽 2 ) = { 𝔽 2 , if ​ t = 2 j ​ for some ​ j ≥ 0 , 0 , otherwise . \mathrm{Ext}^{1,t}_{A}(\mathbb{F}_{2},\mathbb{F}_{2})=\begin{cases}\mathbb{F}_{2},\ \text{if}\ t=2^{j}\ \text{for some}\ j\geq 0,\\ 0,\ \text{otherwise}.\end{cases} Denote by h j h_{j} the generator of 𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2} in the bidegree ( s , t ) = ( 1 , 2 j ) (s,t)=(1,2^{j}) . Adams [ 2 ] proved that the element h j h_{j} survives in the Adams spectral sequence if and only if j ≥ 3 j\geq 3 , resolving the famous Hopf invariant problem. In fact, Adams proved that d 2 ​ ( h j ) = h 0 ​ h j − 1 2 ≠ 0 , for ​ j ≥ 4 . d_{2}(h_{j})=h_{0}h_{j-1}^{2}\neq 0,\ \text{for}\ j\geq 4. The Kervaire invariant element h j 2 h_{j}^{2} lives on the Adams 2-line, which is generated by elements of the form { h i ​ h j } \{h_{i}h_{j}\} that are subject to the relations h i ​ h i + 1 = 0 h_{i}h_{i+1}=0 . Notably, h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} was the last unknown element on the Adams 2-line whose survival in the Adams spectral sequence remained uncertain prior to our work. As a corollary of Theorem 1.4 , we have: Corollary 1.5 . On the Adams 2-line, the only non-trivial elements that survive in the Adams spectral sequence are: h 0 ​ h 2 , h 0 ​ h 3 , h 2 ​ h 4 , h 1 ​ h j ​ ( j ≥ 3 ) , h j 2 ​ ( j ≤ 6 ) . h_{0}h_{2},\ h_{0}h_{3},\ h_{2}h_{4},\ h_{1}h_{j}\ (j\geq 3),\ h_{j}^{2}\ (j\leq 6). Proof. From Adams’s Hopf invariant one differentials, Lin’s computations of the Adams 4-line [ 40 ] , and the Leibniz rule, we know that the only elements that survive to the Adams E 3 E_{3} -page are h 0 ​ h 2 , h 0 ​ h 3 , h 2 ​ h 4 , h 2 ​ h 5 , h 3 ​ h 5 , h 3 ​ h 6 , h 1 ​ h j ​ ( j ≥ 3 ) , h j 2 ​ ( j ≤ 6 ) . h_{0}h_{2},\ h_{0}h_{3},\ h_{2}h_{4},\ h_{2}h_{5},\ h_{3}h_{5},\ h_{3}h_{6},\ h_{1}h_{j}\ (j\geq 3),\ h_{j}^{2}\ (j\leq 6). May [ 45 ] proved that the first three elements and h j 2 h_{j}^{2} for j ≤ 3 j\leq 3 survive. Mahowald–Tangora [ 44 ] proved that h 2 ​ h 5 h_{2}h_{5} supports a nonzero d 3 d_{3} -differential, h 3 ​ h 5 h_{3}h_{5} supports a nonzero d 4 d_{4} -differential, and h 4 2 h_{4}^{2} survives. Isaksen–Wang–Xu [ 29 ] proved that h 3 ​ h 6 h_{3}h_{6} supports a nonzero d 3 d_{3} -differential. Mahowald [ 43 ] proved that h 1 ​ h j h_{1}h_{j} for j ≥ 3 j\geq 3 survive. Barratt–Jones–Mahowald [ 4 ] proved that h 5 2 h_{5}^{2} survives (see [ 56 , 29 ] for alternative proofs). Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel [ 23 ] proved that h j 2 h_{j}^{2} supports nonzero differentials for j ≥ 7 j\geq 7 (the targets of these differentials are still unknown). Finally, by Theorem 1.4 , h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} survives. ∎ Before Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel’s proof on the non-existence of θ j \theta_{j} for j ≥ 7 j\geq 7 , Barratt–Jones–Mahowald [ 3 ] had an inductive argument trying to establish the existence of all θ j \theta_{j} : They proved that if there exists a θ j \theta_{j} satisfying 2 ⋅ θ j = 0 , θ j 2 = 0 2\cdot\theta_{j}=0,\ \theta_{j}^{2}=0 , then there exists a θ j + 1 \theta_{j+1} satisfying 2 ⋅ θ j + 1 = 0 2\cdot\theta_{j+1}=0 . In particular, the problem of whether a θ j + 1 \theta_{j+1} of order 2 exists is known as the strong Kervaire invariant problem. It was known that 2 ⋅ θ j = 0 2\cdot\theta_{j}=0 for j ≤ 5 j\leq 5 (see [ 44 ] for j = 4 j=4 and [ 56 , 29 ] for j = 5 j=5 ). Although we prove that θ 6 \theta_{6} exists in this paper, the following questions are still open: Question 1.6 . Does there exist a θ 6 \theta_{6} that has order 2? Question 1.7 . Does there exist a θ 5 \theta_{5} such that θ 5 2 = 0 \theta_{5}^{2}=0 ? By Barratt–Jones–Mahowald’s theorem [ 3 ] , if the answer to Question 1.7 is positive, then it would imply our Theorem 1.4 and a positive answer to Question 1.6 . We plan to study these questions in a future project. As suggested by the proof of Corollary 1.5 , computation of differentials in the Adams spectral sequence has a long history. See Section 2 of [ 53 ] for a brief summary at all primes and computations at the prime 2 up to around 60-stem. For more recent computations up to around 90-stem, see [ 29 , 28 , 55 , 30 ] . Many methods were introduced to compute differentials in the Adams spectral sequence. We highlight some of the major methods as follows. (1) Multiplicative structure of the Adams E 2 E_{2} -page and the Leibniz rule. By computing the Adams E 2 E_{2} -page with its multiplicative structure via the May spectral sequence, and comparing with Toda’s unstable computations, May [ 45 ] computed all differentials up to the 28-stem. (2) Higher product structure – interactions between Massey products and Toda brackets through Moss’s theorem [ 48 ] . (3) The Mahowald trick [ 44 ] , which translates between differentials and extension problems. By using both Moss’s theorem and the Mahowald trick, Barratt–Mahowald–Tangora [ 44 , 5 ] computed all but one differentials up to the 45-stem; The remaining one was computed by Bruner [ 11 ] using power operations. (4) Comparison with the motivic Adams spectral sequence via the Betti realization functor [ 20 ] . By comparison via the Betti realization functor, Isaksen [ 26 ] gave rigorous arguments for all but one differentials in both the motivic and classical Adams spectral sequence up to the 59-stem; The remaining one was computed by Xu [ 31 ] using the higher Leibniz rule for motivic Massey products. (5) Wang–Xu’s ℝ ​ P ∞ \mathbb{R}P^{\infty} -method [ 53 ] . Using Lin’s algebraic Kahn–Priddy theorem [ 39 ] , Wang–Xu introduced a technique to prove Adams differentials inductively using differentials in subquotients of ℝ ​ P ∞ \mathbb{R}P^{\infty} , and completed computations of Adams differentials up to the 61-stem. (See also [ 54 ] for application of this method on extension problems.) There is a nice geometric application of these differentials in stems 60 and 61. The sphere S 61 S^{61} has a unique smooth structure and it is the last odd dimensional case: The only ones are S 1 , S 3 , S 5 S^{1},\ S^{3},\ S^{5} and S 61 S^{61} . (6) Gheorghe–Isaksen–Wang–Xu’s motivic cofiber of tau method [ 22 , 29 ] . By identifying the algebraicity of the special fiber of a motivic deformation, Gheorghe–Wang–Xu [ 22 ] proved that the motivic Adams spectral sequence of the cofiber of tau is isomorphic to the algebraic Novikov spectral sequence for BP ∗ , which can be completely computed in a large range by Wang’s program [ 52 ] . Then differentials in the classical Adams spectral sequence follow from naturality. Using this method, together with some others, Isaksen–Wang–Xu [ 29 ] computed Adams differentials up to the 90-stem, with only a few exceptions. (7) H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic/filtered spectra method [ 50 , 13 , 18 , 17 ] . The homotopy ring of the H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic sphere [ 50 , 16 ] can be viewed as a tool to encode homotopical information of the classical Adams spectral sequence, in analog to the relation between the homotopy ring of the ℂ \mathbb{C} -motivic sphere [ 26 ] and the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. By studying the H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic sphere, Burklund–Isaksen–Xu [ 17 ] proved a few Adams differentials up to the 90-stem that were left out by Isaksen–Wang–Xu [ 29 ] . Our strategy for proving our main Theorem 1.4 that h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} survives is three-fold. • Lin’s Program. By establishing the theory of noncommutative Gröbner bases for Steenrod algebras [ 36 ] , Lin made computer programs that compute Ext groups in a very effective way. We use Lin’s program to compute the Adams E 2 E_{2} -pages for a vast collection of finite spectra and the maps between them. For a detailed account of the range of Adams E 2 E_{2} data computed, see [ 38 ] . Building on the theory of secondary Steenrod algebras [ 6 , 49 , 19 ] , we also leverage Lin’s program to compute Adams d 2 d_{2} -for certain finite spectra. Details on the d 2 d_{2} -differentials we have computed can also be found in [ 38 ] . Additionally, Lin’s program facilitates the propagation of differentials and extensions by employing the Leibniz rule and the naturality of Adams spectral sequences and extension spectral sequences (Definition 2.1 ). • The Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick. The use of H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic/filtered spectra allows us to discuss elements on the Adams E n E_{n} -page as classes in the homotopy groups of certain synthetic spectra [ 50 , 18 ] . In particular, naturality on synthetic homotopy groups allows us to discuss jump-of-filtration phenomena on any Adams E n E_{n} -page in a rigorous way. Using H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra, we develop the Generalized Leibniz Rule (Theorem 6.1 ) and the Generalized Mahowald Trick (Theorem 6.12 ), which can be viewed as generalizations of the classical Mahowald’s trick and geometric boundary theorem studies by Ravenel, Behrens and others [ 51 , 8 , 41 , 14 ] . We then use them to further propagate differentials from the known ones. With the Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick, Lin’s program becomes even more powerful, enabling the derivation of additional differentials. The program rigorously verifies the conditions of the relevant theorems and generates human-readable proofs [ 37 ] for all differentials computed by the machine. In the Appendix, readers will find tables of Adams differentials that are used in the proof of the main Theorem 1.4 . • The inductive approach and adhoc arguments near stem 126. By studying Barratt–Jones–Mahowald’s inductive approach [ 3 ] in the H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic context, Burklund–Xu (Proposition 7.19 of [ 18 ] ) proved that h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} is a permanent cycle in the classical Adams spectral sequence if and only if λ ​ η ​ θ 5 2 = 0 \lambda\eta\theta_{5}^{2}=0 in the homotopy group of the synthetic sphere. Here λ \lambda is our notation for the synthetic deformation parameter, and θ 5 \theta_{5} is any synthetic homotopy class that is detected by h 5 2 h_{5}^{2} on the Adams E 2 E_{2} -page. There are 105 additive generators in stem 125 of the classical Adams E 2 E_{2} -page – these are the potential targets that could be hit by a nonzero differential from h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} . Using Lin’s program and the inductive approach, we can rule out 101 out of the 105 elements. By further applying the Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick, we can reduce the possibility of a nonzero differential supported by h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} to only one case: A nonzero d 12 d_{12} hits a certain element in stem 125, filtration 14 (see Proposition 7.8 (2)). A necessary condition for this nonzero d 12 d_{12} is an η \eta -extension from stem 124, filtration 10 to stem 125, filtration 14 (see Proposition 7.8 (5)). By a careful inspection of the homotopy groups of the synthetic sphere (and on certain Adams E n E_{n} -pages) near stem 126, we use adhoc arguments to prove that the η \eta -extension in the previous paragraph cannot happen (see Proposition 7.9 ) and conclude that h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} survives in the Adams spectral sequence. Organization. In Section 2, we setup and discuss properties of an extension spectral sequence for a map f : X → Y f:X\to Y between two spectra, whose E 0 E_{0} -page is isomorphic to the direct sum of E ∞ E_{\infty} -pages of the Adams spectral sequences of X X and Y Y , and differentials correspond to f ∗ : π ∗ ​ X → π ∗ ​ Y f_{*}:\pi_{*}X\to\pi_{*}Y , filtered by the Adams filtration. This setup allows us to discuss the jump-of-filtration phenomena rigorously. In Section 3, we recall and discuss certain properties of H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra. In Section 4, we discuss the extension spectral sequence from Section 2 in the context of H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra. In Section 5, we discuss extensions on a classical Adams E n E_{n} -page, defined in terms of H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra. In Section 6, using the language of Sections 4 and 5, we develop the Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick. In Section 7, we use the inductive approach, and ad hoc arguments prove that h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} survives in the Adams spectral sequence. Necessary information of the classical Adams spectral sequence for the final ad hoc arguments is included in the Appendix. Acknowledgement. A Spectral Sequence for Extensions In this paper, we assume that all symbols X , Y , Z , W , … X,Y,Z,W,\dots for spectra represent 2-completed connective spectra of finite type. In particular, their H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -Adams spectral sequences converge strongly. Consider a map f : X → Y f:X\to Y between two spectra and we can construct the following chain complex 0 → π ∗ ​ X → π ∗ ​ f π ∗ ​ Y → 0 0\to\pi_{*}X\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}f}\pi_{*}Y\to 0 whose homology is ker ⁡ ( π ∗ ​ f ) ⊕ coker ⁡ ( π ∗ ​ f ) . \Ker(\pi_{*}f)\oplus\Coker(\pi_{*}f). We encounter f f -extension problems when analyzing the homotopy groups through the E ∞ E_{\infty} -pages of the H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -based Adams spectral sequences. To address these extensions, we introduce the following spectral sequence. Definition 2.1 . For a map f : X → Y f:X\to Y , we define an f f -extension spectral sequence (denoted f f -ESS) as the spectral sequence derived from filtering the above chain complex according to the Adams filtration on π ∗ \pi_{*} . The E 0 E_{0} -page of this spectral sequence is isomorphic to the direct sum of E ∞ E_{\infty} -pages of the Adams spectral sequences of X X and Y Y : E s , t 0 f ≅ E ∞ s , t ​ ( X ) ⊕ E ∞ s , t ​ ( Y ) ⟹ ker ⁡ ( π ∗ ​ f ) ⊕ coker ⁡ ( π ∗ ​ f ) \prescript{f\!}{}{E}_{0}^{s,t}\cong E_{\infty}^{s,t}(X)\oplus E_{\infty}^{s,t}(Y)\Longrightarrow\Ker(\pi_{*}f)\oplus\Coker(\pi_{*}f) where the d 0 d_{0} -differential is induced by f f on the E ∞ E_{\infty} -pages. Differentials in this spectral sequence has the form d n f : E s , t n f → E s + n , t + n n f . d_{n}^{f}:\prescript{f\!}{}{E}_{n}^{s,t}\to\prescript{f\!}{}{E}_{n}^{s+n,t+n}. To prevent confusion between differentials d n d_{n} in the Adams spectral sequence of a spectrum X X and those in the f f -extension spectral sequence, we denote the latter with a superscript, writing them as d n f d_{n}^{f} . In the remainder of the paper, we denote by AF ⁡ ( f ) \mathrm{AF}(f) the Adams filtration of a map f f . Recall that AF ⁡ ( f ) ≥ k \mathrm{AF}(f)\geq k if and only if there exist k k composable maps f 1 , … , f k f_{1},\ldots,f_{k} , each inducing a trivial map in H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -homology, such that f f is homotopic to the composition f k ∘ ⋯ ∘ f 1 f_{k}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1} (see [ 51 , Theorem 2.2.14] for example). Notation 2.2 . Let Z n s , t f ​ ( X ) ⊂ E ∞ s , t ​ ( X ) \prescript{f\!}{}{Z}^{s,t}_{n}(X)\subset E^{s,t}_{\infty}(X) be the subgroup consisting of elements for which the f f -extension differentials d 0 f , … , d n f d^{f}_{0},\dots,d^{f}_{n} vanish. Let B n s , t f ​ ( Y ) ⊂ E ∞ s , t ​ ( Y ) \prescript{f\!}{}{B}^{s,t}_{n}(Y)\subset E^{s,t}_{\infty}(Y) be the subgroup generated by the sum of images of the f f -extension differentials d 0 f , … , d n f d^{f}_{0},\dots,d^{f}_{n} . We define B − 1 s , t f ​ ( Y ) = 0 \prescript{f\!}{}{B}^{s,t}_{-1}(Y)=0 and Z − 1 s , t f ​ ( X ) = E ∞ s , t ​ ( X ) \prescript{f\!}{}{Z}^{s,t}_{-1}(X)=E^{s,t}_{\infty}(X) . By definition we have E n s , t f ≅ Z n − 1 s , t f ​ ( X ) ⊕ ( E ∞ s , t ​ ( Y ) / B n − 1 s , t f ​ ( Y ) ) . \prescript{f\!}{}{E}^{s,t}_{n}\cong\prescript{f\!}{}{Z}^{s,t}_{n-1}(X)\oplus\big(E^{s,t}_{\infty}(Y)/\prescript{f\!}{}{B}^{s,t}_{n-1}(Y)\big). A nonzero d n f d^{f}_{n} differential takes the form d n f : Z n − 1 s , t f ​ ( X ) → E ∞ s + n , t + n ​ ( Y ) / B n − 1 s + n , t + n f ​ ( Y ) . d^{f}_{n}:\prescript{f\!}{}{Z}^{s,t}_{n-1}(X)\to E^{s+n,t+n}_{\infty}(Y)/\prescript{f\!}{}{B}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y). When we write d n f ​ ( x ) = y d^{f}_{n}(x)=y for x ∈ E ∞ s , t ​ ( X ) x\in E_{\infty}^{s,t}(X) and y ∈ E ∞ s + n , t + n ​ ( Y ) y\in E_{\infty}^{s+n,t+n}(Y) , it is understood that x x belongs to Z n − 1 s , t f ​ ( X ) \prescript{f\!}{}{Z}^{s,t}_{n-1}(X) , and d n f ​ ( x ) = y + B n − 1 s + n , t + n f ​ ( Y ) . d^{f}_{n}(x)=y+\prescript{f\!}{}{B}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y). Furthermore, we may sometimes write d n f ​ ( x ) ≡ y mod M d^{f}_{n}(x)\equiv y\mod M for some subgroup M ⊂ E ∞ s + n , t + n ​ ( Y ) M\subset E_{\infty}^{s+n,t+n}(Y) . This means that d n f ​ ( x ) = y + m + B n − 1 s + n , t + n f ​ ( Y ) d^{f}_{n}(x)=y+m+\prescript{f\!}{}{B}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y) for some m ∈ M m\in M . Definition 2.3 . We say there is an f f -extension from x ∈ E ∞ s , t ​ ( X ) x\in E_{\infty}^{s,t}(X) to y ∈ E ∞ s + n , t + n ​ ( Y ) y\in E_{\infty}^{s+n,t+n}(Y) if d n f ​ ( x ) = y d_{n}^{f}(x)=y in the f f -ESS. We say this f f -extension is essential if y y is nontrivial in the E n E_{n} -page of the f f -ESS, or equivalently y ∉ B n − 1 s + n , t + n f ​ ( Y ) . y\notin\prescript{f\!}{}{B}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y). Otherwise we refer to it as inessential. Notation 2.4 . For x ∈ E ∞ s , t ​ ( X ) x\in E_{\infty}^{s,t}(X) , we denote by { x } \{x\} the set of all classes in π t − s ​ X \pi_{t-s}X that are detected by x x . We use [ x ] [x] to refer to a specific class or a general class in { x } \{x\} , with the choice understood from the context. Proposition 2.5 . Remark 2.9 . We do not require the crossings in Definition 2.7 (1)(2) to be essential. A crossing implies the existence of an essential crossing, possibly a shorter one. Proposition 2.10 . An f f -extension from x x to y y has no crossing in the range AF ≥ p \mathrm{AF}\geq p if and only if for all [ x ] ∈ { x } [x]\in\{x\} such that AF ⁡ ( f ⁡ [ x ] ) ≥ p \mathrm{AF}(f[x])\geq p we have f ⁡ [ x ] ∈ { y } f[x]\in\{y\} . In particular, we have (1) An f f -extension from x x to y y has no crossing if and only if for all [ x ] ∈ { x } [x]\in\{x\} we have f ⁡ [ x ] ∈ { y } f[x]\in\{y\} . (2) An f f -extension d n f ​ ( x ) = 0 d_{n}^{f}(x)=0 has no crossing if and only if for all [ x ] ∈ { x } [x]\in\{x\} , AF ⁡ ( f ⁡ [ x ] ) > AF ⁡ ( x ) + n . \mathrm{AF}(f[x])>\mathrm{AF}(x)+n. (3) If AF ⁡ ( f ) = n \mathrm{AF}(f)=n , then all d n f d^{f}_{n} -differentials have no crossing in the f f -ESS. Proof. First we prove the only if part. Assume by contradiction that there exists [ x ] ∈ { x } [x]\in\{x\} such that f ⁡ [ x ] = [ y ′ ] ∉ { y } f[x]=[y^{\prime}]\notin\{y\} is detected by y ′ ∈ E ∞ ​ ( Y ) y^{\prime}\in E_{\infty}(Y) with AF ⁡ ( y ′ ) ≥ p \mathrm{AF}(y^{\prime})\geq p . Then there is an f f extension from x x to y ′ y^{\prime} . By Proposition 2.5 (3), y ′ y^{\prime} (if AF ⁡ ( y ) > AF ⁡ ( y ′ ) \mathrm{AF}(y)>\mathrm{AF}(y^{\prime}) ) or y − y ′ y-y^{\prime} (if AF ⁡ ( y ) = AF ⁡ ( y ′ ) \mathrm{AF}(y)=\mathrm{AF}(y^{\prime}) ) or y y (if AF ⁡ ( y ) < AF ⁡ ( y ′ ) \mathrm{AF}(y)<\mathrm{AF}(y^{\prime}) ) should be hit by a shorter d f d^{f} differential. This shorter differential is a crossing of the f f -extension from x x to y y that hits AF ≥ p \mathrm{AF}\geq p , which is a contradiction. Now we prove the if part. Assume that the f f -extension has a crossing from x ′ x^{\prime} to y ′ y^{\prime} with p ≤ AF ⁡ ( y ′ ) ≤ AF ⁡ ( y ) p\leq\mathrm{AF}(y^{\prime})\leq\mathrm{AF}(y) . Then there exists [ x ′ ] ∈ { x ′ } [x^{\prime}]\in\{x^{\prime}\} such that f ⁡ ( [ x ′ ] ) ∈ { y ′ } f([x^{\prime}])\in\{y^{\prime}\} . Note that [ x ] + [ x ′ ] ∈ { x } [x]+[x^{\prime}]\in\{x\} since AF ⁡ ( x ′ ) > AF ⁡ ( x ) \mathrm{AF}(x^{\prime})>\mathrm{AF}(x) , we have f ⁡ ( [ x ] + [ x ′ ] ) = f ⁡ ( [ x ] ) + f ⁡ ( [ x ′ ] ) = [ y ] + [ y ′ ] ∈ { { y ′ } if ​ AF ​ ( y ′ ) < AF ⁡ ( y ) { y + y ′ } if ​ AF ​ ( y ′ ) = AF ⁡ ( y ) . f([x]+[x^{\prime}])=f([x])+f([x^{\prime}])=[y]+[y^{\prime}]\in\begin{cases}\{y^{\prime}\}&\text{ if }\mathrm{AF}(y^{\prime})<\mathrm{AF}(y)\\ \{y+y^{\prime}\}&\text{ if }\mathrm{AF}(y^{\prime})=\mathrm{AF}(y).\\ \end{cases} However, by assumption we should have f ⁡ ( [ x ] + [ x ′ ] ) ∈ { y } f([x]+[x^{\prime}])\in\{y\} and it is contradictory to the equation above in either case. Hence the main statement is true. Part (1) (2) are special cases of the main statement and Part (3) holds for degree reasons. ∎ Example 2.11 . Then ( d r p , d r q ) : E ∞ ∗ , ∗ ​ ( X ) ⊕ E ∞ ∗ , ∗ ​ ( Y ) → E ∞ ∗ , ∗ ​ ( Z ) ⊕ E ∞ ∗ , ∗ ​ ( W ) (d^{p}_{r},d^{q}_{r}):E_{\infty}^{*,*}(X)\oplus E_{\infty}^{*,*}(Y)\to E_{\infty}^{*,*}(Z)\oplus E_{\infty}^{*,*}(W) induces a map from the f f -ESS to the g g -ESS. Proof. Since E ∗ , ∗ 0 p = E ∗ , ∗ r p \prescript{p\!}{}{E}_{0}^{*,*}=\prescript{p\!}{}{E}_{r}^{*,*} and E ∗ , ∗ 0 q = E ∗ , ∗ r q \prescript{q\!}{}{E}_{0}^{*,*}=\prescript{q\!}{}{E}_{r}^{*,*} , we know that d r p d^{p}_{r} , d r q d^{q}_{r} have no crossings. By Corollary 2.15 , we have d 0 g ∘ d r p = d r q ∘ d 0 f d^{g}_{0}\circ d^{p}_{r}=d^{q}_{r}\circ d^{f}_{0} and therefore ( d r p , d r q ) (d^{p}_{r},d^{q}_{r}) induces a map from E ∗ , ∗ 1 f \prescript{f\!}{}{E}_{1}^{*,*} to E ∗ , ∗ 1 g \prescript{g\!}{}{E}_{1}^{*,*} . Inductively we can apply Corollary 2.15 again and show that ( d r p , d r q ) (d^{p}_{r},d^{q}_{r}) induces a map from E ∗ , ∗ n f \prescript{f\!}{}{E}_{n}^{*,*} to E ∗ , ∗ n g \prescript{g\!}{}{E}_{n}^{*,*} and d n g ∘ d r p = d r q ∘ d n f d^{g}_{n}\circ d^{p}_{r}=d^{q}_{r}\circ d^{f}_{n} for all n n . ∎ Corollary 2.19 . If the composition g ∘ f g\circ f of two maps f : X → Y f:X\to Y , g : Y → Z g:Y\to Z is trivial, and d n f ​ ( x ) = y d_{n}^{f}(x)=y for x ∈ E ∞ s , t ​ ( X ) x\in E_{\infty}^{s,t}(X) and y ∈ E ∞ s + n , t + n ​ ( Y ) y\in E_{\infty}^{s+n,t+n}(Y) , then y y is a permanent cycle in the g g -ESS, i.e., we have d m g ​ ( y ) = 0 d^{g}_{m}(y)=0 for all m ≥ 0 m\geq 0 . Proof. This follows immediately from the following commutative diagram X \textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} f \scriptstyle{f} Y \textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} g \scriptstyle{g} 0 \textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Z \textstyle{Z} and Corollary 2.15 . There is a second short proof using homotopy groups. By Proposition 2.5 , we know that there exists [ x ] ∈ { x } [x]\in\{x\} such that f ⁡ ( [ x ] ) ∈ { y } f([x])\in\{y\} . Let [ y ] = f ⁡ ( [ x ] ) ∈ { y } [y]=f([x])\in\{y\} and we have g ⁡ ( [ y ] ) = g ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( [ x ] ) ) = 0 g([y])=g(f([x]))=0 . Hence y y is a permanent d g d^{g} cycle. ∎ Proposition 2.20 . Suppose that the sequence of spectra X → 𝑓 Y → 𝑔 Z X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z induces a sequence of homotopy groups π ∗ ​ X → π ∗ ​ f π ∗ ​ Y → π ∗ ​ g π ∗ ​ Z \pi_{*}X\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}f}\pi_{*}Y\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}g}\pi_{*}Z that is exact in π ∗ ​ Y \pi_{*}Y . Then all permanent cycles in E ∞ ∗ , ∗ ​ ( Y ) E^{*,*}_{\infty}(Y) of the g g -ESS are boundaries in the f f -ESS. Proof. If we consider the following sequence (2.21) 0 → π ∗ ​ X → π ∗ ​ f π ∗ ​ Y → π ∗ ​ g π ∗ ​ Z → 0 0\to\pi_{*}X\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}f}\pi_{*}Y\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}g}\pi_{*}Z\to 0 and treat it as a chain complex filtered by the Adams filtration, we obtain an extension spectral sequence comprising d f d^{f} and d g d^{g} differentials. The abutment of this spectral sequence, when projected onto the Y Y component, is zero. Therefor, all permanent d g d^{g} -cycles are killed by d f d^{f} -differentials. Again we offer another proof via homotopy groups. Assume that y ∈ E ∞ ∗ , ∗ ​ ( Y ) y\in E^{*,*}_{\infty}(Y) is a permanent d g d^{g} cycle. By the convergence of ESS we know that it detects an g g -torsion [ y ] ∈ { y } [y]\in\{y\} with g ⁡ ( [ y ] ) = 0 g([y])=0 . Hence there exists [ x ] ∈ π ∗ ​ X [x]\in\pi_{*}X such that f ⁡ ( [ x ] ) = [ y ] f([x])=[y] by exactness. Assume that [ x ] [x] is detected by x ∈ E ∞ ∗ , ∗ ​ ( X ) x\in E^{*,*}_{\infty}(X) . Then there exists an f f extension from x x to y y . ∎ Corollary 2.19 and Proposition 2.20 are particularly useful because they provide numerous potential applications whenever we have a cofiber sequence of the form X → 𝑓 Y → 𝑔 C ​ f → ℎ Σ ​ X X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Cf\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X These results can also be implemented in a computer program to facilitate their application. 3. H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra In Section 2 , we explored classical extension problems in the context of commutative diagrams and cofiber sequences of spectra, framed in terms of the extension spectral sequence. The power of the extension spectral sequence can be further enhanced by generalizing and applying it to the category of H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra, a topic we will discuss in Section 4 . In this section, we recall some recent work of Pstragowski [ 50 ] and Appendix A of Burklund–Hahn–Senger [ 16 ] on E E -synthetic spectra, which is strongly motivated by the work of Hu–Kriz–Ormsby [ 25 ] , Isaksen [ 26 ] , Gheorghe–Wang–Xu [ 22 ] , and Gheorghe–Isaksen–Krause–Ricka [ 21 ] on the ℂ \mathbb{C} -motivic spectra. We will specialize some of these results to H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra, refining and highlighting specific properties. The stable, presentable, symmetric monoidal ∞ \infty -category Syn H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{Syn}_{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}} of H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra is constructed in [ 50 ] , along with a functor ν : Sp → Syn H ​ 𝔽 2 , \nu:\mathrm{Sp}\to\mathrm{Syn}_{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}, from the ∞ \infty -category of spectra Sp \mathrm{Sp} . This functor is lax symmetric monoidal and preserves filtered colimits. However, ν \nu does not generally preserve cofiber sequences. Instead, the following holds. Proposition 3.1 ( [ 50 , Lemma 4.23] ) . Suppose that X → 𝑓 Y → 𝑔 Z X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z is a cofiber sequence of spectra. Then ν ​ X → ν ​ f ν ​ Y → ν ​ g ν ​ Z \nu X\xrightarrow{\nu f}\nu Y\xrightarrow{\nu g}\nu Z is a cofiber sequence of H ​ 𝔽 2 {\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}} -synthetic spectra if and only if 0 → H ​ 𝔽 2 ∗ ​ X → H ​ 𝔽 2 ∗ ​ f H ​ 𝔽 2 ∗ ​ Y → H ​ 𝔽 2 ∗ ​ g H ​ 𝔽 2 ∗ ​ Z → 0 0\to{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}_{*}X\xrightarrow{{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}_{*}f}{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}_{*}Y\xrightarrow{{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}_{*}g}{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}_{*}Z\to 0 is a short exact sequence. Definition 3.2 ( [ 50 , Definition 4.6] ) . The bigraded spheres S s , w S^{s,w} are the synthetic spectra defined by Σ s − w ​ ν ​ ( S w ) \Sigma^{s-w}\nu(S^{w}) . Definition 3.3 ( [ 50 , Definition 4.27] ) . The natural comparison map S 0 , − 1 = Σ ​ ν ​ ( S − 1 ) → ν ⁡ ( Σ ​ S − 1 ) = S 0 , 0 S^{0,-1}=\Sigma\nu(S^{-1})\to\nu(\Sigma S^{-1})=S^{0,0} in the category of H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra is denoted by λ ∈ π 0 , − 1 ​ S 0 , 0 \lambda\in\pi_{0,-1}S^{0,0} . We denote by S 0 , 0 / λ S^{0,0}/\lambda the cofiber of λ \lambda . In general for synthetic X X the following λ ∧ i ​ d X : Σ 0 , − 1 ​ X → X . \lambda\wedge id_{X}:\Sigma^{0,-1}X\to X. is a natural transformation and by abuse of notation this map is also denoted by λ \lambda . We denote by X / λ X/\lambda the cofiber of λ \lambda which is equivalent to ( S 0 , 0 / λ ) ∧ X (S^{0,0}/\lambda)\wedge X . Remark 3.4 . For every n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , S 0 , 0 / λ n S^{0,0}/\lambda^{n} is an E ∞ E_{\infty} -object in Syn H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{Syn}_{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}} (see [ 15 , Appendix B, C] for example). Remark 3.5 . The element λ \lambda can be viewed as a parameter of a categorical deformation on Syn H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{Syn}_{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}} (see [ 50 ] ). Sp 2 ∧ \textstyle{\textup{Sp}^{\wedge}_{2}} Syn H ​ 𝔽 2 \textstyle{\mathrm{Syn}_{\mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} λ − 1 \scriptstyle{\lambda^{-1}} generic fiber m ​ o ​ d ​ λ \scriptstyle{mod~\lambda} special fiber 𝒟 ⁡ ( A ∗ ​ -Comod ) \textstyle{\mathcal{D}(A_{*}\textup{-Comod})} Inverting λ \lambda , the generic fiber is equivalent to the classical category of 2-completed spectra Sp 2 ∧ \textup{Sp}^{\wedge}_{2} . Modding out by λ \lambda , the special fiber is equivalent to Hovey’s derived category [ 24 ] of comodules over the mod 2 dual Steenrod algebra 𝒟 ⁡ ( A ∗ ​ -Comod ) \mathcal{D}(A_{*}\textup{-Comod}) . Historically, this synthetic deformation is motivated by, and analogous to, the motivic deformation conjectured by Isaksen and proven by Gheorghe–Wang–Xu [ 22 ] : Specifically, there exists a map τ : S 0 , − 1 ^ → S 0 , 0 ^ \tau:\widehat{S^{0,-1}}\rightarrow\widehat{S^{0,0}} between p-completed motivic spheres. This element τ \tau can be viewed as a parameter of a categorical deformation on the category of cellular objects over S 0 , 0 ^ \widehat{S^{0,0}} . Sp p ∧ \textstyle{\textup{Sp}^{\wedge}_{p}} S 0 , 0 ^ ​ -Mod \textstyle{\widehat{S^{0,0}}\textup{-Mod}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} τ − 1 \scriptstyle{\tau^{-1}} generic fiber m ​ o ​ d ​ τ \scriptstyle{mod~\tau} special fiber 𝒟 ⁡ ( B ​ P ∗ ​ B ​ P ​ -Comod ) \textstyle{\mathcal{D}(BP_{*}BP\textup{-Comod})} Inverting τ \tau , the generic fiber is equivalent to the classical category of p-completed spectra Sp p ∧ \textup{Sp}^{\wedge}_{p} . Modding out by τ \tau , the special fiber is equivalent to Hovey’s derived category of comodules over the Hopf algebroid B ​ P ∗ ​ B ​ P BP_{*}BP . Theorem 3.6 ( [ 16 , Theorem A.8] ) . The synthetic Adams spectral sequence for ν ​ X \nu X E s , t , w 2 syn ​ ( ν ​ X ) ⟹ π t − s , w ​ ( ν ​ X ) \prescript{\mathrm{syn}\!}{}{E}_{2}^{s,t,w}(\nu X)\Longrightarrow\pi_{t-s,w}(\nu X) has an E 2 E_{2} -page E ∗ , ∗ , ∗ 2 syn ​ ( ν ​ X ) ≅ E 2 ∗ , ∗ ​ ( X ) ⊗ 𝔽 2 ​ [ λ ] \prescript{\mathrm{syn}\!}{}{E}_{2}^{*,*,*}(\nu X)\cong E_{2}^{*,*}(X)\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}[\lambda] with differentials d r : E s , t , w r syn → E s + r , t + r − 1 , w r syn . d_{r}:\prescript{\mathrm{syn}\!}{}{E}_{r}^{s,t,w}\to\prescript{\mathrm{syn}\!}{}{E}_{r}^{s+r,t+r-1,w}. An element in E 2 s , t ​ ( X ) E_{2}^{s,t}(X) is viewed as having tridegree ( s , t , t ) (s,t,t) in E s , t , t 2 syn ​ ( ν ​ X ) \prescript{\mathrm{syn}\!}{}{E}_{2}^{s,t,t}(\nu X) , and λ \lambda is in tridegree ( 0 , 0 , − 1 ) (0,0,-1) . Given a classical Adams differential d r cl ​ ( x ) = y d_{r}^{\mathrm{cl}}(x)=y , the corresponding synthetic differential is d r ​ ( x ) = λ r − 1 ​ y d_{r}(x)=\lambda^{r-1}y , which is λ \lambda -linear, and all synthetic Adams differentials arise in this way. Remark 3.7 . Here, we use a third grading of the synthetic Adams E 2 E_{2} -page that differs from [ 16 ] but more compatible with [ 17 ] . Specifically, we view elements in E 2 s , t ​ ( X ) E_{2}^{s,t}(X) as having tridegree ( s , t , t ) (s,t,t) , instead of ( s , t , s ) (s,t,s) as in [ 16 ] . This choice ensures that differentials and maps preserve the third degree, which proves convenient for the extension spectral sequence in the H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic category in Section 4 . Theorem 3.8 ( [ 16 , Theorem A.1] ) . The synthetic Adams spectral sequence for ν ​ X \nu X is isomorphic to the λ \lambda -Bockstein spectral sequence (with no sign difference because we are working over 𝔽 2 {\mathbb{F}}_{2} here). More specifically, we have (1) E 2 s , t ​ ( X ) ≅ π t − s , t ​ ( ν ​ X / λ ) E_{2}^{s,t}(X)\cong\pi_{t-s,t}(\nu X/\lambda) . (2) If we have a classical Adams differential d r ​ x = y d_{r}x=y for x ∈ E 2 s , t ​ ( X ) ≅ π t − s , t ​ ( ν ​ X / λ ) , x\in E_{2}^{s,t}(X)\cong\pi_{t-s,t}(\nu X/\lambda), then x x admits a lift to π t − s , t ​ ( ν ​ X / λ r − 1 ) \pi_{t-s,t}(\nu X/\lambda^{r-1}) whose image under the τ \tau -Bockstein ν ​ X / λ r − 1 → Σ 1 , − r + 1 ​ ν ​ X / λ \nu X/\lambda^{r-1}\to\Sigma^{1,-r+1}\nu X/\lambda is equal to d r ​ ( x ) d_{r}(x) . Remark 3.9 . The isomorphism with the λ \lambda -Bockstein spectral sequence in Theorem 3.8 and the correspondence of differentials between the classical and synthetic Adams spectral sequences in Theorem 3.6 can be interpreted as manifestations of the rigidity of the synthetic Adams spectral sequence. This type of rigidity was first observed by Hu–Kriz–Ormsby [ 25 ] and Isaksen [ 26 ] in the context of the motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence, and it was utilized extensively in the work of Gheorghe–Wang–Xu [ 22 ] . Subsequently, Burklund–Xu [ 18 ] uncovered the rigidity of the motivic Cartan–Eilenberg spectral sequence and performed computations based on this property. See also [ 27 ] , [ 13 ] for applications of this type of rigidity. Together with Remark 3.5 , H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra can be seen as a categorification of the classical Adams spectral sequence, just as cellular motivic spectra can be viewed a categorification of the classical Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. Remark 3.21 . When AF ⁡ ( f ) > 1 \mathrm{AF}(f)>1 , it is often more advantageous to work with C ​ f ~ C\tilde{f} rather than C ​ f ^ C\hat{f} , as the λ \lambda -Bockstein spectral sequence for C ​ f ~ C\tilde{f} corresponds to a modified Adams spectral sequence, which differs from the classical one but may provide additional information. On the other hand, given the equivalence C ​ f ^ ≃ ν ​ C ​ f C\hat{f}\simeq\nu Cf from Notation 3.19 , the rigidity Theorems 3.6 and 3.8 imply that the synthetic Adams spectral sequence for C ​ f ^ C\hat{f} is isomorphic to the λ \lambda -Bockstein spectral sequence, and aligns with the classical Adams spectral sequence for C ​ f Cf . Since the computational data used in this work is based on the classical Adams spectral sequence, we defer the exploration of C ​ f ~ C\tilde{f} to future work. To illustrate this in more detail, consider the example of the synthetic lift η 3 ~ ∈ π 3 , 6 \tilde{\eta^{3}}\in\pi_{3,6} of η 3 ∈ π 3 \eta^{3}\in\pi_{3} , which has AF = 3 \mathrm{AF}=3 . We can examine several λ \lambda -multiples of η 3 ~ \tilde{\eta^{3}} : λ ​ η 3 ~ ∈ π 3 , 5 , η 3 ^ = λ 2 ​ η 3 ~ ∈ π 3 , 4 , ν ⁡ ( η 3 ) = λ 3 ​ η 3 ~ ∈ π 3 , 3 . \lambda\tilde{\eta^{3}}\in\pi_{3,5},\ \ \hat{\eta^{3}}=\lambda^{2}\tilde{\eta^{3}}\in\pi_{3,4},\ \ \nu(\eta^{3})=\lambda^{3}\tilde{\eta^{3}}\in\pi_{3,3}. Now, consider the synthetic 2-cell complexes formed by taking the cofibers of these maps: C ⁡ ( η 3 ~ ) , C ⁡ ( λ ​ η 3 ~ ) , C ⁡ ( λ 2 ​ η 3 ~ ) ≃ C ⁡ ( η 3 ^ ) ≃ ν ⁡ ( C ​ η 3 ) , C ⁡ ( λ 3 ​ η 3 ~ ) ≃ C ​ ν ​ ( η 3 ) . C(\tilde{\eta^{3}}),\ C(\lambda\tilde{\eta^{3}}),\ C(\lambda^{2}\tilde{\eta^{3}})\simeq C(\hat{\eta^{3}})\simeq\nu(C\eta^{3}),\ C(\lambda^{3}\tilde{\eta^{3}})\simeq C\nu(\eta^{3}). The rigidity Theorems 3.6 and 3.8 apply only to C ⁡ ( η 3 ^ ) C(\hat{\eta^{3}}) , as it is equivalent to ν ⁡ ( C ​ η 3 ) \nu(C\eta^{3}) . In this case, its synthetic Adams E 2 E_{2} -page is λ \lambda -free over Ext A ∗ , ∗ ​ ( C ​ η 3 ) \mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{*,*}(C\eta^{3}) ; in particular the identity element from the top cell has tridegree ( s , t , w ) = ( 0 , 4 , 4 ) (s,t,w)=(0,4,4) . There is a correspondence of differentials between the classical and synthetic Adams spectral sequence, and a synthetic differential generates λ \lambda -torsion corresponding to its length. For C ⁡ ( η 3 ~ ) , C ⁡ ( λ ​ η 3 ~ ) C(\tilde{\eta^{3}}),C(\lambda\tilde{\eta^{3}}) and C ⁡ ( λ 3 ​ η 3 ~ ) C(\lambda^{3}\tilde{\eta^{3}}) , their synthetic Adams E 2 E_{2} -pages remain λ \lambda -free, but the identity element from the top cell has tridegree ( s , t , w ) = ( 0 , 4 , 6 ) , ( 0 , 4 , 5 ) , ( 0 , 4 , 7 ) (s,t,w)=(0,4,6),(0,4,5),(0,4,7) respectively. Synthetic Extensions The extension spectral sequence introduced in Section 2 can be generalized for H ​ 𝔽 2 \mathrm{H}{\mathbb{F}}_{2} -synthetic spectra. Therefore, we can omit B r − 1 s + r , t + r − 1 ​ ( X ) B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X) and simply write d r δ n ​ ( x ) = λ r − n − 1 ​ y d^{\delta_{n}}_{r}(x)=\lambda^{r-n-1}y when r ≥ n + 1 r\geq n+1 , and d r δ n ​ ( λ n + 1 − r ​ x ) = y d^{\delta_{n}}_{r}(\lambda^{n+1-r}x)=y when r ≤ n + 1 r\leq n+1 . ∎ Remark 4.9 . The d r δ ​ ( x ) d^{\delta}_{r}(x) we calculated in Proposition 4.6 may be inessential. Corollary 4.10 . For x , y , δ x,y,\delta in Proposition 4.6 we always have (4.11) d r δ ​ ( λ a ​ x ) = λ a + r − n − 1 ​ y d^{\delta}_{r}(\lambda^{a}x)=\lambda^{a+r-n-1}y if 0 ≤ a ≤ n 0\leq a\leq n and 0 ≤ a + r − n − 1 < m − n 0\leq a+r-n-1<m-n (the differential is trivial if a a exceeds this range). Remark 4.12 . As indicated in the proof, the right-hand side of equation ( 4.11 ) (considered as a subset of Z ∞ s + r , t + r − 1 ​ ( X ) Z_{\infty}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X) ) is a coset of B r − 1 s + r , t + r − 1 ​ ( X ) B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X) which is the same as the value of the classical Adams differential d r ​ ( x ) = y d_{r}(x)=y . This implies that the equation ( 4.11 ) holds if and only if d r ​ ( x ) = y d_{r}(x)=y . Therefore the δ \delta -ESS encodes the same information as the classical Adams spectral sequence. A crossing of d r ​ ( x ) = y d_{r}(x)=y on the E n + 1 E_{n+1} -page The emphasis on a crossing occurring “on the E n + 1 E_{n+1} -page" in Definition 4.14 may seem counter-intuitive. The Adams differential d r ​ ( x ) = y d_{r}(x)=y has a crossing on the E n + 1 E_{n+1} -page if and only if the corresponding δ n \delta_{n} -extension d r δ n ​ ( x ) = λ r − n − 1 ​ y d^{\delta_{n}}_{r}(x)=\lambda^{r-n-1}y for δ n : ν ​ X / λ n → Σ 1 , − n ​ ν ​ X \delta_{n}:\nu X/\lambda^{n}\to\Sigma^{1,-n}\nu X has a crossing. Proof. By Corollary 4.10 , we see that this crossing corresponds to the classical Adams differential d r − a − b ​ ( x ′ ) = y ′ . d_{r-a-b}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}. ∎ Remark 4.17 . Since the synthetic Adams E ∞ E_{\infty} -page contains more information than the classical Adams E ∞ E_{\infty} -page, the ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extensions similarly provide more information compared to the classical f f -extensions. 6. The Generalized Leibniz Rule and Generalized Mahowald Trick Now, we introduce the theorem of the Generalized Leibniz Rule, a valuable tool for computing new Adams differentials. Theorem 6.1 (Generalized Leibniz Rule) . Let f : X → Y f:X\to Y be a map between two classical spectra. Suppose that 2 ≤ n ≤ r 2\leq n\leq r , e ⁡ ( f ) ≤ m ≤ n − 2 + e ⁡ ( f ) e(f)\leq m\leq n-2+e(f) , l ≥ e ⁡ ( f ) l\geq e(f) , and we have x ∈ Z r − 1 s , t ​ ( X ) , y ∈ Z r − 1 − m + e ⁡ ( f ) s + m , t + m ​ ( Y ) x\in Z_{r-1}^{s,t}(X),\hskip 14.22636pty\in Z_{r-1-m+e(f)}^{s+m,t+m}(Y) x ∞ ∈ Z ∞ s + r , t + r − 1 ​ ( X ) , y ∞ ∈ Z ∞ s + r + l , t + r + l − 1 ​ ( Y ) x_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X),\hskip 14.22636pty_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+l,t+r+l-1}(Y) and the following conditions hold: (1) d r ​ ( x ) = x ∞ d_{r}(x)=x_{\infty} , (2) d m f , E n ​ ( x ) = y d_{m}^{f,E_{n}}(x)=y , (3) d l f , E ∞ ​ ( x ∞ ) = y ∞ d_{l}^{f,E_{\infty}}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty} , (4) the differential in (1) has no crossing on the E n E_{n} -page or (2) has no crossing. (5) the differential in (3) has no crossing. Then we have an Adams differential d r + l − m ​ ( y ) = y ∞ . d_{r+l-m}(y)=y_{\infty}. Proof. Consider the commutative diagram of synthetic spectra Σ 0 , e ⁡ ( f ) ​ ν ​ X / λ n − 1 \textstyle{\Sigma^{0,e(f)}\nu X/\lambda^{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} f ^ n − 1 \scriptstyle{\hat{f}_{n-1}} δ X \scriptstyle{\delta_{X}} ν ​ Y / λ n − 1 \textstyle{\nu Y/\lambda^{n-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} δ Y \scriptstyle{\delta_{Y}} Σ 1 , − n + 1 + e ⁡ ( f ) ​ ν ​ X \textstyle{\Sigma^{1,-n+1+e(f)}\nu X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} f ^ \scriptstyle{\hat{f}} Σ 1 , − n + 1 ​ ν ​ Y . \textstyle{\Sigma^{1,-n+1}\nu Y.} By condition (1) and Proposition 4.6 , we have (6.2) d r δ X ​ ( x ) = λ r − n ​ x ∞ . d^{\delta_{X}}_{r}(x)=\lambda^{r-n}x_{\infty}. By conditions (2) and (3), and Definition 5.4 , we have (6.3) d m f ^ n − 1 ​ ( x ) = λ m − e ⁡ ( f ) ​ y d^{\hat{f}_{n-1}}_{m}(x)=\lambda^{m-e(f)}y and d l f ^ ​ ( x ∞ ) = λ l − e ⁡ ( f ) ​ y ∞ , d^{\hat{f}}_{l}(x_{\infty})=\lambda^{l-e(f)}y_{\infty}, which implies (6.4) d l f ^ ​ ( λ r − n ​ x ∞ ) = λ r + l − n − e ⁡ ( f ) ​ y ∞ . d^{\hat{f}}_{l}(\lambda^{r-n}x_{\infty})=\lambda^{r+l-n-e(f)}y_{\infty}. Applying Proposition 4.16 and Proposition 5.10 to conditions (4) and (5), we know that the differential in ( 6.2 ) or ( 6.3 ) has no crossing, and that the differential in ( 6.4 ) has no crossing. Therefore, we can apply Corollary 2.15 and obtain d r + l − m δ Y ​ ( λ m − e ⁡ ( f ) ​ y ) = λ r + l − n − e ⁡ ( f ) ​ y ∞ . d^{\delta_{Y}}_{r+l-m}(\lambda^{m-e(f)}y)=\lambda^{r+l-n-e(f)}y_{\infty}. By Remark 4.12 , this is equivalent to d r + l − m ​ ( y ) = y ∞ . d_{r+l-m}(y)=y_{\infty}. ∎ Remark 6.5 . We can further generalize Theorem 6.1 by using the conditions in Theorem 2.12 rather than those Corollary 2.15 . We leave this generalization to the reader. We emphasize that the no-crossing conditions in Theorem 6.1 , the Generalized Leibniz Rule, are crucial. We first demonstrate the strength of the Generalized Leibniz Rule with Example 6.7 , which uses a d 2 d_{2} -differential to prove a d 3 d_{3} -differential in the classical Adams spectral sequence. Additionally, Example 6.8 shows that, without the no-crossing condition, the conclusion could be false. Remark 6.6 . A version of the Generalized Leibniz Rule without the no-crossing conditions is presented in the synthetic setting in Chua’s work [ 19 , Theorem 12.9] . However, there is no doubt that this version is incorrect. Indeed, Example 6.8 is a counter-example to Chua’s theorem. For further details, see Remark 6.10 . Example 6.7 . We show that the Generalized Leibniz Rule can be used to prove the following classical Adams differential d 3 ​ ( h 2 ​ h 5 ) = h 0 ​ p , d_{3}(h_{2}h_{5})=h_{0}p, using the classical Hopf invariant one differential d 2 ​ ( h 5 ) = h 0 ​ h 4 2 , d_{2}(h_{5})=h_{0}h_{4}^{2}, and the ( f , E 3 ) (f,E_{3}) -extension d 2 f , E 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 2 ) = h 0 ​ p , d_{2}^{f,E_{3}}(h_{0}h_{4}^{2})=h_{0}p, for the map f = ν : S 3 → S 0 f=\nu:S^{3}\rightarrow S^{0} from Example 5.8 . Specifically, we have: n = 2 , r = 2 , s = 1 , t = 35 , m = 1 , l = 2 , e ⁡ ( f ) = 1 , n=2,\ r=2,\ s=1,\ t=35,\ m=1,\ l=2,\ e(f)=1, and x = h 5 ∈ Ext A 1 , 32 ≅ Z 1 1 , 35 ​ ( S 3 ) , y = h 2 ​ h 5 ∈ Ext A 2 , 36 ≅ Z 1 2 , 36 ​ ( S 0 ) , x=h_{5}\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{1,32}\cong Z_{1}^{1,35}(S^{3}),\ y=h_{2}h_{5}\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{2,36}\cong Z_{1}^{2,36}(S^{0}), x ∞ = h 0 ​ h 4 2 ∈ Ext A 3 , 33 ≅ Z ∞ 3 , 36 ​ ( S 3 ) , y ∞ = h 0 ​ p ∈ Ext A 5 , 38 ≅ Z ∞ 5 , 38 ​ ( S 0 ) . x_{\infty}=h_{0}h_{4}^{2}\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{3,33}\cong Z_{\infty}^{3,36}(S^{3}),\ y_{\infty}=h_{0}p\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{5,38}\cong Z_{\infty}^{5,38}(S^{0}). In terms of the conditions in Theorem 6.1 , we have: (1) d 2 ​ ( h 5 ) = h 0 ​ h 4 2 d_{2}(h_{5})=h_{0}h_{4}^{2} , (2) d 1 f , E 2 ​ ( h 5 ) = h 2 ​ h 5 d_{1}^{f,E_{2}}(h_{5})=h_{2}h_{5} , from Example 5.8 (1), (3) d 2 f , E ∞ ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 2 ) = h 0 ​ p d_{2}^{f,E_{\infty}}(h_{0}h_{4}^{2})=h_{0}p , from Example 5.8 (3), (4) (a) the differential in (1) has no crossing, from Remark 4.17 , and (b) the differential in (2) has no crossing, from Example 5.13 (1), (5) the differential in (3) has no crossing, from Example 5.13 (3). Since all conditions of Theorem 6.1 are satisfied, we conclude that there is a classical Adams differential d 3 ​ ( h 2 ​ h 5 ) = h 0 ​ p . d_{3}(h_{2}h_{5})=h_{0}p. Example 6.8 . We show that, without the no crossing conditions, the Generalized Leibniz Rule could lead to incorrect conclusions. Consider the map f = 2 : S 0 → S 0 f=2:S^{0}\rightarrow S^{0} , and the classical Hopf invariant one differential d 2 ​ ( h 4 ) = h 0 ​ h 3 2 . d_{2}(h_{4})=h_{0}h_{3}^{2}. Set n = 2 , r = 2 , s = 1 , t = 16 , m = 1 , l = 2 , e ⁡ ( f ) = 1 , n=2,\ r=2,\ s=1,\ t=16,\ m=1,\ l=2,\ e(f)=1, and x = h 4 ∈ Ext A 1 , 16 ≅ Z 1 1 , 16 ​ ( S 0 ) , y = h 0 ​ h 4 ∈ Ext A 2 , 17 ≅ Z 1 2 , 17 ​ ( S 0 ) , x=h_{4}\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{1,16}\cong Z_{1}^{1,16}(S^{0}),\ y=h_{0}h_{4}\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{2,17}\cong Z_{1}^{2,17}(S^{0}), x ∞ = h 0 ​ h 3 2 ∈ Ext A 3 , 17 ≅ Z ∞ 3 , 17 ​ ( S 0 ) , y ∞ = 0 ∈ Ext A 5 , 19 ≅ Z ∞ 5 , 19 ​ ( S 0 ) . x_{\infty}=h_{0}h_{3}^{2}\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{3,17}\cong Z_{\infty}^{3,17}(S^{0}),\ y_{\infty}=0\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{5,19}\cong Z_{\infty}^{5,19}(S^{0}). In terms of the conditions in Theorem 6.1 , we have: (1) d 2 ​ ( h 4 ) = h 0 ​ h 3 2 d_{2}(h_{4})=h_{0}h_{3}^{2} , (2) d 1 f , E 2 ​ ( h 4 ) = h 0 ​ h 4 d_{1}^{f,E_{2}}(h_{4})=h_{0}h_{4} , since the product of h 4 h_{4} by h 0 h_{0} is h 0 ​ h 4 h_{0}h_{4} in Ext, (3) d 2 f , E ∞ ​ ( h 0 ​ h 3 2 ) = 0 d_{2}^{f,E_{\infty}}(h_{0}h_{3}^{2})=0 , from Example 5.8 (4), (4) (a) the differential in (1) has no crossing, from Remark 4.17 , and (b) the differential in (2) has no crossing, for degree reasons, (5) the differential in (3) has a crossing: d 1 f , E ∞ ​ ( d 0 ) = h 0 ​ d 0 , d_{1}^{f,E_{\infty}}(d_{0})=h_{0}d_{0}, from Example 5.13 (4). For condition ( 5 ) (5) , if there were no crossing for the differential in ( 3 ) (3) , then the Generalized Leibniz Rule would imply a classical Adams differential d 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 ) = 0 , d_{3}(h_{0}h_{4})=0, which is incorrect. This example illustrates that the no-crossing conditions are essential when applying the Generalized Leibniz Rule. Remark 6.9 . As noted in Example 5.8 (4) and Example 5.13 (4), the essential ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extension: d 1 f , E ∞ ​ ( d 0 ) = h 0 ​ d 0 , d_{1}^{f,E_{\infty}}(d_{0})=h_{0}d_{0}, is a crossing for both the ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extension, d 2 f , E ∞ ​ ( h 0 ​ h 3 2 ) = 0 , d_{2}^{f,E_{\infty}}(h_{0}h_{3}^{2})=0, and the inessential ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extension: d 2 f , E ∞ ​ ( h 0 ​ h 3 2 ) = h 0 ​ d 0 . d_{2}^{f,E_{\infty}}(h_{0}h_{3}^{2})=h_{0}d_{0}. This indicates that, in Example 6.8 , if we were to disregard the no-crossing condition (5), and apply the Generalized Leibniz Rule to these two cases of the ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extensions, we would arrive at two conflicting classical statements: d 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 ) = 0 , d 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 ) = h 0 ​ d 0 . d_{3}(h_{0}h_{4})=0,\ d_{3}(h_{0}h_{4})=h_{0}d_{0}. Remark 6.10 . In Chua’s work [ 19 , Theorem 12.9] , it is stated that for a synthetic map α : X → Y \alpha:X\rightarrow Y , and an element x ∈ π ∗ , ∗ ​ X / λ x\in\pi_{*,*}X/\lambda , there exists a differential from a maximal α \alpha -extension of x x to a maximal α \alpha -extension of d r ​ ( x ) d_{r}(x) . According to [ 19 , Definition 12.5] , a maximal α \alpha -extension of x ′ x^{\prime} is defined as α ⁡ [ x ′ ] \alpha[x^{\prime}] , where [ x ′ ] [x^{\prime}] represents a lift of x ′ x^{\prime} to the E r ′ E_{r^{\prime}} -page, chosen such that α ⁡ [ x ′ ] \alpha[x^{\prime}] is the most λ \lambda -divisible among all such lifts. In the context of Example 6.8 , let r = 2 , r ′ = ∞ r=2,r^{\prime}=\infty , α = [ h 0 ] : S 0 , 1 → S 0 , 0 , \alpha=[h_{0}]:S^{0,1}\rightarrow S^{0,0}, and consider x = h 4 x=h_{4} in Ext, with x ′ = d 2 ​ ( x ) = h 0 ​ h 3 2 x^{\prime}=d_{2}(x)=h_{0}h_{3}^{2} . As noted in Example 5.8 (4), the ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extension: d 2 f , E ∞ ​ ( h 0 ​ h 3 2 ) = 0 d_{2}^{f,E_{\infty}}(h_{0}h_{3}^{2})=0 is equivalent to the existence of a synthetic homotopy class [ h 0 ​ h 3 2 ] [h_{0}h_{3}^{2}] in π 14 , 17 ​ S 0 , 0 \pi_{14,17}S^{0,0} , such that [ h 0 ​ h 3 2 ] ⋅ [ h 0 ] = 0 ​ in ​ π 14 , 18 ​ S 0 , 0 . [h_{0}h_{3}^{2}]\cdot[h_{0}]=0\ \text{in}\ \pi_{14,18}S^{0,0}. Similarly, the inessential ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extension: d 2 f , E ∞ ​ ( h 0 ​ h 3 2 ) = h 0 ​ d 0 d_{2}^{f,E_{\infty}}(h_{0}h_{3}^{2})=h_{0}d_{0} implies the existence of another synthetic homotopy class [ h 0 ​ h 3 2 ] [h_{0}h_{3}^{2}] in π 14 , 17 ​ S 0 , 0 \pi_{14,17}S^{0,0} , such that [ h 0 ​ h 3 2 ] ⋅ [ h 0 ] = [ h 0 ​ d 0 ] ​ in ​ π 14 , 18 ​ S 0 , 0 . [h_{0}h_{3}^{2}]\cdot[h_{0}]=[h_{0}d_{0}]\ \text{in}\ \pi_{14,18}S^{0,0}. Between these two lifts of h 0 ​ h 3 2 h_{0}h_{3}^{2} , the first [ h 0 ​ h 3 2 ] [h_{0}h_{3}^{2}] is clearly the maximal [ h 0 ] [h_{0}] -extension according to Chua’s definition [ 19 , Definition 12.5] . Consequently, the incorrect version of the Generalized Leibniz Rule in [ 19 , Theorem 12.9] , would lead to an incorrect conclusion: d 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 ) = 0 . d_{3}(h_{0}h_{4})=0. Next, we discuss the Generalized Mahowald Trick. In order to apply the Generalized Leibniz Rule, we need to provide a method for computing extensions on specific Adams E k E_{k} -pages, such as the ( f , E 3 ) (f,E_{3}) -extension d 2 f , E 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 2 ) = h 0 ​ p d_{2}^{f,E_{3}}(h_{0}h_{4}^{2})=h_{0}p in Example 6.7 . This is provided by Theorem 6.12 (the Generalized Mahowald Trick). The crux of the proof of the Generalized Mahowald Trick lies in the following lemma. Lemma 6.11 (May [ 46 ] ) . Let X → Y → Z → Σ ​ X X\to Y\to Z\to\Sigma X and X ′ → Y ′ → Z ′ → Σ ​ X ′ X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\to Z^{\prime}\to\Sigma X^{\prime} be distinguished triangles of (synthetic) spectra. By smashing these distinguished triangles together, we obtain the following commutative diagram of cofiber sequences: X ∧ X ′ \textstyle{X\wedge X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Y ∧ X ′ \textstyle{Y\wedge X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Z ∧ X ′ \textstyle{Z\wedge X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} X ∧ Y ′ \textstyle{X\wedge Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Y ∧ Y ′ \textstyle{Y\wedge Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Z ∧ Y ′ \textstyle{Z\wedge Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} X ∧ Z ′ \textstyle{X\wedge Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Y ∧ Z ′ \textstyle{Y\wedge Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Z ∧ Z ′ \textstyle{Z\wedge Z^{\prime}} If a ∈ π n ​ ( X ∧ Z ′ ) a\in\pi_{n}(X\wedge Z^{\prime}) and b ∈ π n ​ ( Y ∧ Y ′ ) b\in\pi_{n}(Y\wedge Y^{\prime}) map to the same element in π n ​ ( Y ∧ Z ′ ) \pi_{n}(Y\wedge Z^{\prime}) , then there exists c ∈ π n ​ ( Z ∧ X ′ ) c\in\pi_{n}(Z\wedge X^{\prime}) such that (1) b b and c c map to the same element in π n ​ ( Z ∧ Y ′ ) \pi_{n}(Z\wedge Y^{\prime}) , and (2) a a and c c map to the same element via boundary maps in π n − 1 ​ ( X ∧ X ′ ) \pi_{n-1}(X\wedge X^{\prime}) . Proof. The proof follows directly from [ 46 , Lemma 4.6] . In fact, consider V V in Axiom TC3 of [ 46 , Section 4] and the corresponding commutative diagram. By [ 46 , Lemma 4.6] we know that V V is the pull back of the following diagram. V \textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} ⌟ \scriptstyle{\lrcorner} Y ∧ Y ′ \textstyle{Y\wedge Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} X ∧ Z ′ \textstyle{X\wedge Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} Y ∧ Z ′ \textstyle{Y\wedge Z^{\prime}} Since a a and b b map to the same element in π n ​ ( Y ∧ Z ′ ) \pi_{n}(Y\wedge Z^{\prime}) , we know that we can find v ∈ V v\in V such that v v maps to a a in π n ​ ( X ∧ Z ′ ) \pi_{n}(X\wedge Z^{\prime}) and b b in π n ​ ( Y ∧ Y ′ ) \pi_{n}(Y\wedge Y^{\prime}) . Then we let c = j 3 ​ ( v ) ∈ π n ​ ( Z ∧ X ′ ) c=j_{3}(v)\in\pi_{n}(Z\wedge X^{\prime}) , where j 3 : V → Z ∧ X ′ j_{3}:V\rightarrow Z\wedge X^{\prime} is the map in the commutative diagram in Axiom TC3 of [ 46 , Section 4] . This lemma follows from the commutativity of the diagram. ∎ Theorem 6.12 (Generalized Mahowald Trick) . By definition, this is equivalent to d n f , E n + m + 1 + e ⁡ ( h ) ​ x ≡ y mod B r ′ s + n + l , t + n + l − 1 ​ ( Y ) . d^{f,E_{n+m+1+e(h)}}_{n}x\equiv y\mod B_{r^{\prime}}^{s+n+l,t+n+l-1}(Y). ∎ We refer to Theorem 6.12 as the Generalized Mahowald Trick, as this approach was first utilized by Mahowald and his collaborators in various works (see, for example, [ 44 ] ), particularly in the case where m 1 = l 1 = 0 m_{1}=l_{1}=0 . The synthetic setting advances this method by allowing for the consideration of cases where m 1 > 0 , l 1 > 0 m_{1}>0,l_{1}>0 as well. Remark 6.17 . Several versions of classical generalizations of Mahowald’s original trick appear in the literature and are often referred to as geometric boundary theorems. Notable examples include the works of Behrens [ 8 ] , and Ma [ 41 ] . Remark 6.18 . A synthetic generalization of the Mahowald Trick, again lacking any no-crossing conditions, is also presented in Chua’s work [ 19 , Theorem 12.11] . This version is also incorrect for similar reasons. Example 6.19 . This is a classical Adams d 2 d_{2} -differential for S 0 / ν S^{0}/\nu , and can be computed using Lin’s computer program via the method of the secondary Steenrod algebra [ 6 , 49 , 19 ] . (3) (a) The differential in (1) has no crossing, as it is a d 0 d_{0} -differential, and (b) the differential in (2) has no crossing, from Remark 4.17 . (4) d 0 g , E 2 ​ ( h 0 ​ p ) = h 0 ​ p ​ [ 0 ] d_{0}^{g,E_{2}}(h_{0}p)=h_{0}p[0] , as h 0 ​ p h_{0}p is not divisible by h 2 h_{2} in Ext. Since all conditions of Theorem 6.12 are satisfied, we conclude that x = h 0 ​ h 4 2 x=h_{0}h_{4}^{2} survives to the E 3 E_{3} -page, and there is an ( f , E 3 ) (f,E_{3}) -extension: d 2 f , E 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 2 ) = h 0 ​ p , d_{2}^{f,E_{3}}(h_{0}h_{4}^{2})=h_{0}p, as promised. The outcome of the Generalized Mahowald Trick, as stated in Theorem 6.12 , is an f f -extension on a specific Adams page, such as the ( f , E 3 ) (f,E_{3}) -extension d 2 f , E 3 ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 2 ) = h 0 ​ p d_{2}^{f,E_{3}}(h_{0}h_{4}^{2})=h_{0}p in Example 5.8 (2) and Example 6.19 . In practice, the source of an ( f , E r ) (f,E_{r}) -extension may survive to later Adams pages, prompting interest in the ( f , E > r ) (f,E_{>r}) -extensions, such as the ( f , E ∞ ) (f,E_{\infty}) -extension d 2 f , E ∞ ​ ( h 0 ​ h 4 2 ) = h 0 ​ p d_{2}^{f,E_{\infty}}(h_{0}h_{4}^{2})=h_{0}p in Example 5.8 (3), which is applied in Example 6.7 . Suppose that we have an ( f , E r ) (f,E_{r}) -extension d n f , E r ​ ( x ) = y d^{f,E_{r}}_{n}(x)=y , where x ∈ Z r − 1 s , t ​ ( X ) x\in Z_{r-1}^{s,t}(X) and y ∈ Z r − 1 − n + e ⁡ ( f ) s + n , t + n ​ ( Y ) y\in Z_{r-1-n+e(f)}^{s+n,t+n}(Y) . Proof. Next, we prove the second part of the proposition. There are two scenarios where this differential is not induced by ( ρ X , ρ Y ) (\rho_{X},\rho_{Y}) . The first case occurs when λ a ​ x ′ \lambda^{a}x^{\prime} is not in the image of ρ X \rho_{X} at all, which is equivalent to x ′ ∉ Z r − 1 − a s + a , t + a ​ ( X ) x^{\prime}\notin Z_{r-1-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X) . Corollary 6.23 . Suppose r ≥ r ′ r\geq r^{\prime} and there exists an ( f , E r ′ ) (f,E_{r^{\prime}}) -extension d n f , E r ′ ​ ( x ) = y d^{f,E_{r^{\prime}}}_{n}(x)=y for x ∈ Z r − 1 s , t ​ ( X ) x\in Z_{r-1}^{s,t}(X) and y ∈ Z r ′ − 1 − n + e ⁡ ( f ) s + n , t + n ​ ( Y ) y\in Z_{r^{\prime}-1-n+e(f)}^{s+n,t+n}(Y) . Proof of the main theorem In this section, we present the proof of our main Theorem 1.4 . Theorem 7.1 (Theorem 1.4 ) . The element h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} survives to the E ∞ E_{\infty} -page in the Adams spectral sequence. We first recall the following theorem, known as the inductive method, originally by Barratt–Jones–Mahowald [ 3 ] and later extended to the H 𝔽 2 \mathbb{F}_{2} -synthetic setting by Burklund–Xu [ 18 , Proposition 7.19] . Notation 7.2 . Let θ 5 = [ h 5 2 ] \theta_{5}=[h_{5}^{2}] represent any synthetic homotopy class in π 62 , 62 + 2 ​ S 0 , 0 \pi_{62,62+2}S^{0,0} detected by h 5 2 h_{5}^{2} in the Adams E 2 E_{2} -page. For convenience, we use the same notation, θ 5 \theta_{5} , to denote its image in π 62 , 62 + 2 ​ S 0 , 0 / λ r \pi_{62,62+2}S^{0,0}/\lambda^{r} via the map S 0 , 0 → S 0 , 0 / λ r S^{0,0}\rightarrow S^{0,0}/\lambda^{r} for all r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 . Similarly, let η = [ h 1 ] ∈ π 1 , 1 + 1 ​ S 0 , 0 \eta=[h_{1}]\in\pi_{1,1+1}S^{0,0} . Theorem 7.3 (Barratt–Jones–Mahowald, Burklund–Xu) . (1) The element h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} survives to the E r + 3 E_{r+3} -page of the classical Adams spectral sequence if and only if for some θ 5 \theta_{5} , λ ​ η ​ θ 5 2 = 0 ​ in ​ π 125,125 + 4 ​ S 0 , 0 / λ r + 1 . \lambda\eta\theta_{5}^{2}=0\ \text{in}\ \pi_{125,125+4}S^{0,0}/\lambda^{r+1}. (2) In particular, h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} is a permanent cycle in the classical Adams spectral sequence if and only if for some θ 5 \theta_{5} , λ ​ η ​ θ 5 2 = 0 ​ in ​ π 125,125 + 4 ​ S 0 , 0 . \lambda\eta\theta_{5}^{2}=0\ \text{in}\ \pi_{125,125+4}S^{0,0}. Remark 7.4 . The statement in Theorem 7.3 (1) was originally stated as η ​ θ 5 2 = 0 ​ in ​ π 125,125 + 5 ​ S 0 , 0 / λ r \eta\theta_{5}^{2}=0\ \text{in}\ \pi_{125,125+5}S^{0,0}/\lambda^{r} in [ 18 , Proposition 7.19] . Upon inspection, π 125,125 + 5 ​ S 0 , 0 \pi_{125,125+5}S^{0,0} doesn’t contain any λ \lambda -torsion classes, so this is equivalent to the version we stated, which is more consistent with the statement in part (2). Remark 7.5 . Theorem 7.3 is proved using the quadratic construction on a map from the mod 2 Moore spectrum to the sphere spectrum, where the restriction on the bottom cell is θ 5 \theta_{5} . Therefore, it is necessary to use a θ 5 \theta_{5} of order 2. Notably, [ 56 , 29 ] confirms that all classical θ 5 \theta_{5} ’s indeed have order 2. Furthermore, from Proposition 3.11 and an analysis of the differentials in the classical Adams spectral sequence, we find that π 62 , 62 + 2 ​ S 0 , 0 \pi_{62,62+2}S^{0,0} doesn’t contain any λ \lambda -torsion. Consequently, all synthetic θ 5 \theta_{5} ’s also have order 2, making it valid to apply Theorem 7.3 to any θ 5 \theta_{5} . Additionally, since the proof of Theorem 7.3 shows that the expression λ ​ η ​ θ 5 2 \lambda\eta\theta_{5}^{2} corresponds to the total differential δ 1 : S 0 , 0 / λ → S 1 , − 1 \delta_{1}:S^{0,0}/\lambda\rightarrow S^{1,-1} on h 6 2 h_{6}^{2} , the value of the expression λ ​ η ​ θ 5 2 \lambda\eta\theta_{5}^{2} is consistent for every choice of θ 5 \theta_{5} . (Note that our grading for the triangulation translation functor is smashing with S 1 , 0 S^{1,0} , which is consistent with [ 16 , Appendix A] but is different from [ 18 , Section 7] , so the target of δ 1 \delta_{1} is S 1 , − 1 S^{1,-1} . ) We pay special attention to the following three elements on the classical Adams E 2 E_{2} -page (see Figure 9 and Tables 7 , 9 , 5 , 6 in the Appendix): h 1 ​ h 4 ​ x 109 , 12 \displaystyle h_{1}h_{4}x_{109,12} ∈ Ext A 14,125 + 14 , \displaystyle\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{14,125+14}, x 126 , 8 , 4 + x 126 , 8 \displaystyle x_{126,8,4}+x_{126,8} ∈ Ext A 8,126 + 8 , \displaystyle\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{8,126+8}, h 0 2 ​ x 124 , 8 \displaystyle h_{0}^{2}x_{124,8} ∈ Ext A 10,124 + 10 , \displaystyle\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{10,124+10}, g 4 ​ Δ ​ h 1 ​ g \displaystyle g^{4}\Delta h_{1}g ∈ Ext A 25,125 + 25 . \displaystyle\in\mathrm{Ext}_{A}^{25,125+25}. Appendix: The classical Adams spectral sequence in the range 122 ≤ t − s ≤ 127 , s ≤ 25 122\leq t-s\leq 127,s\leq 25 We provide a brief overview of Lin’s computer program for computing Adams differentials and extensions. The program’s functionality for propagating differentials and extensions relies on the following data: • The Adams E 2 E_{2} -pages of a collection of CW spectra. • Maps between these E 2 E_{2} -pages. • Adams d 2 d_{2} -differentials for certain CW spectra. • There are three manually added differentials: d 5 ​ h 0 24 ​ h 6 = h 0 2 ​ P 6 ​ d 0 d_{5}h_{0}^{24}h_{6}=h_{0}^{2}P^{6}d_{0} and d 6 ​ h 0 55 ​ h 7 = h 0 2 ​ x 126 , 60 d_{6}h_{0}^{55}h_{7}=h_{0}^{2}x_{126,60} in the Adams spectral sequence of S 0 S^{0} (from the image of J J ), and d 3 ​ v 2 16 = β 5 ​ g d_{3}v_{2}^{16}=\beta^{5}g in the Adams spectral sequence of 𝑡𝑚𝑓 \mathit{tmf} , derived from power operations (Bruner–Rognes [ 12 ] ). Detailed descriptions of these data are available in [ 37 ] . Lin’s program extends these results by computing additional Adams differentials using tools such as the Leibniz rule, naturality, the Generalized Leibniz Rule, and the Generalized Mahowald Trick. All computed differentials and extensions are accessible via interactive plots [ 35 ] . Moreover, the proofs provided in [ 37 ] offer more information than the interactive plots. These proofs include numerous disproofs of potential differentials, even for cases where the differentials remain unresolved. For example, consider x 126 , 21 ∈ E 2 21,126 + 21 ​ ( S 0 ) . x_{126,21}\in E_{2}^{21,126+21}(S^{0}). The spectral sequence plot for S 0 S^{0} shows that x 126 , 21 x_{126,21} survives to the E 4 E_{4} -page, but the value of d 4 ​ ( x 126 , 21 ) d_{4}(x_{126,21}) undetermined. By analyzing the proofs in [ 37 ] , we observe that many potential values for d 4 ​ ( x 126 , 21 ) d_{4}(x_{126,21}) have been ruled out. Consequently, we conclude: d 4 ​ ( x 126 , 21 ) = x 125 , 25 , 2 + x 125 , 25 + g 4 ​ Δ ​ h 1 ​ g + possibly ​ d 0 2 ​ e 0 ​ g ​ B 4 . d_{4}(x_{126,21})=x_{125,25,2}+x_{125,25}+g^{4}\Delta h_{1}g+\text{possibly }d_{0}^{2}e_{0}gB_{4}.
